  If I don't specify the time when the action happened, can I use "I've seen it" instead of saying "I saw it"? Is it right?
    ↳ **@TheVannalle** (👍28 · 2016-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ljKhIxcrhwk&lc=UgiYYllZUFWI2XgCoAEC.84NkYpSHfP988IJyhWQrXC
      +Matheus Mascarenhas The difference lies on what are you trying to say. If you saw a movie and have an opinion about it  'saw' explains it. If you've lived a situation that repeats itself "have seen it' explains it better. For example: I saw you in that bar X I've seen you in that bar. The first on tells you were there one time for sure. The second suggests you were there more than once.
    ↳ **@drogadepc** (👍1 · 2018-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ljKhIxcrhwk&lc=UgiYYllZUFWI2XgCoAEC.84NkYpSHfP98iUct7iQNRB
      Matheus Mascarenhas if you only saw a movie once than no, you can't say I've seen.

      "I've seen Star Wars movies these days".

  If you are going to teach a particular genre of communication or a particular variant of English (General American I guess), you ought to be explicit about that - rather than demeaning fully functional variants of English. 
  
  One would expect as much from a person claiming enough proficiency to actually teach the language. It is really backwards to think of removing regional variance as "cleaning up our language".
  
  (On a side note: many regional variants of English have the possibility of expressing modalities not possible in Standard English grammar.)

  AAVE (or African American Vernacular) is a unique dialect of American English with it's own grammar rules, not just "stupid mistakes" as you simply put it. There is nothing wrong with "I ain't that good" it's just not standard English. Obviously, I understand as an English teacher you would need to the standard English, just dismissing AAVE as just "stupid mistake" is off putting and just plain incorrect. I also really cringe when I hear people describe the area where poor people of color live as "the ghetto". Can we stop doing that, god!

  Problem with "If I was you..." was common because of declination "to be" in past tense - I WAS and you/he/she/it were xd ... So were is the logic of using "if I WERE (seriously? Why? XD) you". We hadn't any other choice but learn it by the heart. 
  Thank you for the video :)

   A: You probably think that they ate all the pizza?

    ↳ **@foxomega425** (👍1 · 2016-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7FA8_NZrGSE&lc=UgjxLbqLJv5Kk3gCoAEC.8CFfr4-w3Wc8EciyBg64mX
      Saying "You are too generous" implies that the generosity is an excess.
      Saying "You are so generous" implies that they are just very generous.
      Both are grammatically correct.

**@FeazzyHD** (👍0 · 2013-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7FA8_NZrGSE&lc=UgwvD7rpY3hNXqI8sT14AaABAg
  Side note: Too can also be used in another way. I'll explain with a sample dialogue.
  
  A: What is your favorite color?
  B: My favorite color is blue.
  A: Really? Me too!
   
  Character A is implying that blue is also their favorite color.
  Too sum up, "Too" can be the same as "also", but saying "me also" would sound very weird.
    ↳ **@styno2010** (👍1 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7FA8_NZrGSE&lc=UgwvD7rpY3hNXqI8sT14AaABAg.8Gbe_PbTTUw9V_FWFPuvPk
      Look at the board… ‘’Me too’’ can be written as ‘’Me as well’.

  I actually have a question. Do we usually use 'Eat' or 'Have' soup, or both are fine?
    ↳ **@tommytruth7595** (👍0 · 2017-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7FA8_NZrGSE&lc=UgjKaA38Jpv8H3gCoAEC.8UYduASwQpo8Utn0bhDBPD
      It is very common to say, "have."   As in, "Have some soup."    Or, "I had some soup for lunch."      Notice the word "some" with both of those, it is common to say that.    "Eat some soup" would be more of a command, at least that is how we would take it if we heard that.    But you certainly could say, "I ate (some) soup for lunch."

  Assume (casual usage)= “without tangible evidence, or to be reluctant and take the element of evidence for granted”. eg) take up position, expect, believe, think.
  Presume (formal usage)= “Based on previous evidence, because the evidence recurred in the past ”. eg) arrogant and overconfident presumptions or presupposed.

  The definition of "presume" that James gives in this video is incorrect. Presuming something doesn't mean that there is any prior evidence. The "pre-" part means "before", but it implies that you are taking something as given *before* taking into account any evidence. "Presumed innocent" means that you are treated as if you are innocent before the verdict is reached.

      +Bluepearl405 He has a great idea about using your fingers to guide your eyes. In fact, I had never thought of using one finger on each side of the line. I think any method might be good, try them and find the one that works for you. Be careful, if your teacher sees you using your finger, they might take the book away from you. We had a reading expert from New Zealand speak with educators here in North Dakota. She explained that if the child is using their finger, it is because the material is too advanced. We needed an expert to tell us that bit of expertise. I never would have thought of that myself.

  Your vids are great, I almost watch them all, but I wanted to ask you to explain the differences between "each other and one another" because these phrases sometimes made me be mistaken ... Thank you, regards :)
    ↳ **@danamonightbird2957** (👍0 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tg6zAkYRAH4&lc=UggdJWRl883wP3gCoAEC.7-H0Z7-8uNp9s4rcz6teQo
      'Each other' is less formal, and is used more. 'One another' is more formal, and is used less in comparison. And 'Each Other' is more applicable when talking about only two people or things. Using 'one another' is more relevant when there are more than two people or things.

**@ChristianHDmsc** (👍1 · 2013-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tg6zAkYRAH4&lc=Ugx1cpOVrXRuDOWKOHp4AaABAg
  I have a question....
  "how much time" is the same as "how long"...?
  please answer me teacher =)
    ↳ **@danamonightbird2957** (👍0 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tg6zAkYRAH4&lc=Ugx1cpOVrXRuDOWKOHp4AaABAg.8Gc6v4rEj-L9s4oCwfYyv9
      There is no difference in meaning but it is more common to use "How long" it can sound less formal and many speakers find it more natural. "How much time" it is often used when asking about a shorter length of time. You might use "how much time" to ask for the amount of time it takes to cook something. And you might use how much time to ask for the estimated length of time it takes to do a task. For example:
      How much does it take to cook rice?
      How much time will it take to clean the house?

  is there any difference between: ''I did say'' or '' I said'' ?????
    ↳ **@danamonightbird2957** (👍0 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tg6zAkYRAH4&lc=UgyUks602yaR_J5UR8V4AaABAg.8Gbj9y8gNqy9s4iiihN9UR
      "I said" is standard. "I did say" is more insisting (we sometimes use do, dose, did, followed by the bare infinitive of the main verb to express emphasis).
      Example:
      A: [After a long conversation] Well, that's about it. 
      B: You said you'd tell me everything you knew about it. 
      A: I did tell you everything about it.

  It's Baconmaster92 here. While speaking, both are interchangable, though while writing, "for example" is used when there are a lot of types, for example: There are many types of vegetables, for example Lettuce, Carrots, Potatoes. 
  For instance is used when there are many possible occurences. For example: You can eat a lot of different vegetables here, for instance you can eat Lettuce, carrots, potatoes.
  This difference is only present in certain english dialects though.

  - Learn by= remeber easyly and well.
  - Ace something= you are good at test/report/essay.
  - Teacher's pet= the teacher likes and loves the student (in a good way).
  - Play hooky (also can be for work)= you should be somewhere and you have no reason for a break.
  - Cut class= not go to the school.
  - Flunk out= be expelled from the school.

  In this case he used the comparative form. "nearER to" means that someone/something is getting closer to other thing. For example he said "close to 5 million people have seen this videos, by the time you see it it will be nearer to 10 billion" meaning that a few months later this video had  an approximate of 5 million views, but now it has got more, so the amount of views are getting closer to 10 million, it will be "more near" to 10m than to 5m. I hope that helps. xx

**@ВиталийСлонов-ч4ъ** (👍1 · 2017-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4wqI-UW1P0I&lc=Ugi2C8phj60T03gCoAEC
  Granted, unless we are talking about physical closeness, the adjective 'near' can be used like a preposition, with or without 'to'. E.g. I live near the station. 
  However, when we are *not talking about physical closeness, 'near to' is often preferred. E.g. I came very near to being offered the job. I was getting cold so I moved near to the fire. I was near to panic as I realized that I was trapped. People near to retirement need to know their pension funds are sufficient. They are near to solving the puzzle.
  The comparative and superlative 'nearer' and 'nearest' are generally used with 'to', though 'to' can be dropped in an informal style. E.g. Come and sit nearer (to) me. Who's the person sitting nearest (to) the door? 
  To sum up, we must sometimes distinguish between physical and figurative senses when it comes to using 'near', mustn't we? Concur?
  Thank you for the video, James.

  Love your vids, James!!  But don't forget other prepositional phrases with 'blow':  'Blowout' can mean a huge party, or a hairdo at a salon. ex., "Hey Sally!  Did you just get a blowout/get your hair blown out? - "Why, yes Patsy, I'm going to Zed's huge blowout in the barn tonight!"   Also, ' to blow over' can mean something strong which has recently subsided, as in a storm or an argument. And don't forget "blown away" meaning "really impressed", as in "I was blown away by James' English lesson"

**@lcozzarelli** (👍0 · 2014-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bwdhlrhziSA&lc=UgwbG923UEIe66wcbHx4AaABAg
  Also "to blow (someone/something) off" means to ignore a person/thing or to not show up for a planned meeting with that person, e.g., "Lydia was supposed to meet me at 7:45 for the 8pm show, but she blew me off."  (I think one would say "blew me out" in the UK, but it's "blew me off" in the US).  You can also blow things off, e.g.,  "I blew off my homework to go bike riding with Mark yesterday, but now I'm regretting it."

      I'm not Hadar, but I think I can help you. The flap T in English as in the words better, water, etc. is very similar to the R in Arabic. As the R in Arabic, the flap t is a trill sound, where the tip of the tongue touches the roof of
      the mouth.

**@learner3898** (👍170 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DxfQFioD3SA&lc=UgwZvhjmM-Sv3xkysXJ4AaABAg
  Ten advanced words for ielts speaking to improve your score :
  1.Exacerbate (v).- to make something worse. 
  (Collocations- problem ,situation, impact, effect.) 
  2. Mitigate (v). - make less severe, less unpleasant. 
  (colloc.-problem, impact, effect, suffering) 
  3.Unsurmountable (adj.)- impossible to solve. 
  (colloc. -barrier, problem, challenge). 
  4.An uphill struggle - difficult /challenging but possible. 
  (colloc. -task, project, game). 
  5.Apprehensive (adj.) - worried or nervous about a bad result.
  (Colloc.-apprehensive about...)
  6.Disgruntled (adj.) - unhappy and disappointed /dissatisfied.
  7.Bewildered (adj.) - confused. 
  (colloc. - thoroughly, utterly, bewildered by...). 
  8.To do my utmost - to try my best. 
  9.Inept (adj.) - not good at your job /showing a lack of skill. 
  (colloc. -very, rather, totally, politically, socially, inept at) 
  10.Impeccable (adj.) - perfect without fault. 
  (colloc. - Job, behavior, manners, English)
  Enjoy learning...

  Exacerbate= make sth worse
  Mitigate= make less unpleasant 
  To do my upmost= try my best
  Disgruntled= unhappy, disappointed, dissatisfied 
  Bewildered= confused
  An uphill struggle= difficult problem but it doesn’t mean that cannot solve
  Unsurmountable= impossible to solve
  Tricky= difficult 
  Turn out, come out= finish 
  Impeccable= perfect
  Apprehensive = worried, scared abt a bad result
  Inept= not good at sth, showing a lack skill
  Laborious= difficult

  Exacerbate problem/effect ~ worse problem/effect
  Mitigate
  Unsurmoutable problem/ challenge (-) ~ imposible to solve
  An uphill struggle ~ difficlut but possible
  Apprehensive (adj) ~ fear about the upcoming future
  Disgrunted (adj) ~ unhappy and unsatisfied/ disappointed
  Tobe utterly bewildered by sth (adj) ~ be confused by sth
  To do my upmost ~ to try my best
  Totally inept at sth (v) ~ not good at your job, lack of skills
  Impeccable (adj) job/behavior ~ perfect without fault

**@md.sifatussabbirsohan9120** (👍1 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DxfQFioD3SA&lc=UgwNStUtlwWXltG55o94AaABAg
  At the top of my head, the carbon footprint caused by folks all around the world exacerbates the problem of climate change, and various steps have been taken to mitigate this issue. To be upfront, it is a challenging task, but it is not unsurmountable. On top of that, it has always been an uphill struggle and quite apprehensive. At the end of the day, the regulations must be impeccable to function correctly.

**@DatNguyen-fr7oz** (👍0 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DxfQFioD3SA&lc=UgzfEoqzXvLXvJqXcnt4AaABAg
  ADVANCE VOCABULARIES FOR IELTS
  
  Ten advanced words for ielts speaking to improve your score :
  
  1.Exacerbate (v).- to make something worse. 
  (Collocations- problem ,situation, impact, effect.) 
  
  => THE PROBLEM OF CLIMATE CHANGE IS EXACERBATED BY PEOPLE FLYING TOO MUCH AND CREATING CARBON EMISSION
  
  2. Mitigate (v). - make less severe, less unpleasant. 
  (colloc.-problem, impact, effect, suffering) 
  
  => WE COULD MITIGATE THE IMPACT OF CLIMATE CHANGE BY FLYING LESS
  
  3.Unsurmountable (adj.)- impossible to solve. 
  (colloc. -barrier, problem, challenge). 
  
  => ALTHOUGH CLIMATE CHANGE IS A CHALLENGING PROBLEM, IT'S NOT UNSURMOUNTABLE TO SOLVE
  
  4.An uphill struggle - difficult /challenging but possible. 
  (colloc. -task, project, game). 
  
  => WRITING MY NEW BOOK HAS BEEN AN UPHILL STRUGGLE
  
  5.Apprehensive (adj.) - worried or nervous about a bad result.
  (Colloc.-apprehensive about...)
  
  => INITIALLY , I WAS REALLY APPREHENSIVE ABOUT MY DRIVING TEST, BUT IN THE END, IT TURNED OUT REALLY WELL
  
  6.Disgruntled (adj.) - unhappy and disappointed /dissatisfied.
  
  =>THE CUSTOMER SERVICE WAS HORRIBLE, I  FELT RATHER DISGRUNTLED
  
  7.Bewildered (adj.) - confused. 
  (colloc. - thoroughly, utterly, bewildered by...). 
  
  =>  I WAS UTTERLY BEWILDERED BY MY FRIEND'S DECISION TO QUIT HIS HIGH PAID JOB
  
  8.To do my utmost - to try my best. 
  
  => THE AUTHORITY HAVE PRODUCED A GOOD ECONOMIC PLAN AND I AM SURE THEY WILL DO THEIR UTMOST TO IMPLEMENT IT.
  
  9.Inept (adj.) - not good at your job /showing a lack of skill. 
  (colloc. -very, rather, totally, politically, socially, inept at) 
  
  
  => MY BOSS LIKES TO DESIGN HIS OWN PP, BUT BETWEEN YOU AND ME, HE'S RATHER INEPT AT IT.
  
  
  
  10.Impeccable (adj.) - perfect without fault. 
  (colloc. - Job, behavior, manners, English)
  
  => MY FRIEND LIVES IN AMERICA, AND SHE SPEAKS IMPECCABLE ENGLISH.
